超越有效性:在冲突影响、资源有限环境下持续开展纤维病毒病 暴发 的环境消毒

Beyond Efficacy: Sustaining Environmental Disinfection During Filovirus Disease Outbreaks in Conflict-affected, Resource-limited Settings
作者： Klinger Soares Faíco-Filho
摘要中文翻译摘要 综述目的 纤维病毒(包括本迪古约病毒)通过接触体液及被体液污染的表面和物品传播,因此环境消毒是感染预防与控制(IPC)的核心。本综述重新审视纤维病毒消毒策略。在实践现场响应中,有效消毒剂的广泛类别及其目标浓度已基本确立,而决定性问题是在现场约束条件下的实施与可持续性。以 2026 年刚果民主共和国(DRC)和乌干达的本迪古约病毒病 暴发 为例。最新进展 近期实验室和现场研究表明,有机基质和多孔表面会显著降低实际应用效果,热带高温环境下氯溶液可快速失效,且氯源的选择涉及储存与稳定性的权衡。本迪古约病毒的消毒数据仍极为有限,因此实践多基于扎伊尔埃博拉病毒推断。全球调查显示,低收入医疗机构的 IPC 监测最为薄弱,而对先前东部刚果民主共和国 暴发 的建模表明,冲突与不信任直接降低响应质量。总结 虽然针对本迪古约病毒的种特异性有效性数据仍有待补充,但未解决的主要挑战并非发现新的病毒灭活剂,而是在 暴发 条件下已证实消毒方法的交付、监测与可持续性。将消毒视为一项从响应首日即资源化并可量化的实施与质量改进问题,是当前紧急情况亟待采取的行动优先事项。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
纤维病毒(包括本迪古约病毒)通过接触体液及被体液污染的表面和物品传播,因此环境消毒是感染预防与控制(IPC)的核心。
证据基础与方法
本综述重新审视纤维病毒消毒策略。在实践现场响应中,有效消毒剂的广泛类别及其目标浓度已基本确立,而决定性问题是在现场约束条件下的实施与可持续性。
共识与结论
以 2026 年刚果民主共和国(DRC)和乌干达的本迪古约病毒病 暴发 为例。近期实验室和现场研究表明,有机基质和多孔表面会显著降低实际应用效果,热带高温环境下氯溶液可快速失效,且氯源的选择涉及储存与稳定性的权衡。
争议与缺口
本迪古约病毒的消毒数据仍极为有限,因此实践多基于扎伊尔埃博拉病毒推断。全球调查显示,低收入医疗机构的 IPC 监测最为薄弱,而对先前东部刚果民主共和国 暴发 的建模表明,冲突与不信任直接降低响应质量。
科研与实践启示
将消毒视为一项从响应首日即资源化并可量化的实施与质量改进问题,是当前紧急情况亟待采取的行动优先事项。

Beyond Efficacy: Sustaining Environmental Disinfection During Filovirus Disease Outbreaks in Conflict-affected, Resource-limited Settings

Authors: Klinger Soares Faíco-Filho
Original abstract (English)
Abstract Purpose of Review Filoviruses, including Bundibugyo virus, spread through contact with body fluids and with surfaces and materials soiled by those fluids, so environmental disinfection is central to infection prevention and control (IPC). This review reframes filovirus disinfection. For practical field response the broad classes of effective agents and their target concentrations are reasonably well established, and the decisive problem is the implementation and sustainability of disinfection under field constraints. The 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda serves as the anchoring example. Recent Findings Recent laboratory and field work shows that organic matrix and porous surfaces substantially reduce real-world efficacy, that chlorine solutions can degrade quickly under tropical heat, and that the choice of chlorine source involves a storage-versus-stability trade-off. Disinfection data specific to Bundibugyo virus remain very limited, so practice extrapolates from Zaire ebolavirus. Global surveys show that IPC monitoring is weakest in low-income facilities, and modeling of prior eastern DRC outbreaks shows that conflict and mistrust directly degrade response quality. Summary Although species-specific efficacy data for Bundibugyo virus are still desirable, the major unresolved challenge is not the discovery of new virucides but the delivery, monitoring and sustainability of proven disinfection under outbreak conditions. Treating disinfection as an implementation and quality-improvement problem, resourced and measured from the first day of response, is an urgent and operationally actionable priority that the current emergency brings into focus.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Filoviruses, including Bundibugyo virus, spread through contact with body fluids and with surfaces and materials soiled by those fluids, so environmental disinfection is central to infection prevention and control (IPC).
Evidence base and method
This review reframes filovirus disinfection. For practical field response the broad classes of effective agents and their target concentrations are reasonably well established, and the decisive problem is the implementation and sustainability of disinfection under field constraints.
Consensus and conclusions
The 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda serves as the anchoring example. Recent laboratory and field work shows that organic matrix and porous surfaces substantially reduce real-world efficacy, that chlorine solutions can degrade quickly under tropical heat, and that the choice of chlorine source involves a storage-versus-stability trade-off.
Controversies and gaps
Disinfection data specific to Bundibugyo virus remain very limited, so practice extrapolates from Zaire ebolavirus. Global surveys show that IPC monitoring is weakest in low-income facilities, and modeling of prior eastern DRC outbreaks shows that conflict and mistrust directly degrade response quality.
Research and practice implications
Treating disinfection as an implementation and quality-improvement problem, resourced and measured from the first day of response, is an urgent and operationally actionable priority that the current emergency brings into focus.

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

世界卫生组织(WHO)——刚果民主共和国丛林病毒病疫情国际卫生条例(IHR)紧急委员会第二次会议:临时建议

Second meeting of the IHR Emergency Committee on the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo – Temporary recommendations - World Health Organization (WHO)
新闻简报世界卫生组织(WHO)与非洲疾控中心(Africa CDC)对向刚果民主共和国(DRC)分配埃博拉病毒疫苗疫苗表示欢迎,并呼吁采取紧急且以社区为主导的行动,以遏制当前丛林病毒病疫情。此前,国际卫生条例(IHR)紧急委员会就该议题召开了第二次会议,会议于2026年8月20日举行。丛林病毒是埃博拉病毒科的成员,当前正在通过临时建议进行管理。尽管现有证据中未提供具体病例或死亡人数,但WHO强调需立即采取遏制措施。应对措施包括疫苗疫苗分配及与当地社区合作,以应对这一卫生紧急事件。当前局势仍在调查中,随着暴发的持续监测,可能需要进一步行动。
查看新闻五要素
时间
国际卫生条例(IHR)紧急委员会就刚果民主共和国丛林病毒病疫情召开第二次会议的时间为2026年8月20日,相关新闻稿及声明发布于2026年8月6日、2026年7月31日及2026年8月24日。
地点与对象
该事件以刚果民主共和国(DRC)为中心,重点关注丛林病毒病暴发。受影响人群包括DRC境内面临或已暴露于丛林病毒的个体。
事件
WHO与非洲疾控中心对向DRC分配埃博拉病毒疫苗疫苗表示欢迎,并呼吁采取紧急且以社区为主导的行动,以遏制DRC境内的埃博拉病毒病暴发。WHO已确认此为DRC境内丛林病毒病疫情,并发布相关声明。
规模、影响与风险
暴发涉及丛林病毒,其属于埃博拉病毒科。WHO已发布临时建议以应对疫情,但现有证据中未提供具体病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。WHO将该风险描述为需要紧急遏制措施。
应对、状态与不确定性
WHO与非洲疾控中心正积极参与应对,包括疫苗疫苗分配及倡导社区主导行动。由于建议为临时性,表明当前局势仍在调查中,可能需要进一步行动。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Second meeting of the IHR Emergency Committee on the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo – Temporary recommendations - World Health Organization (WHO)

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) and the Africa CDC have welcomed the allocation of Ebola vaccines to the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and called for urgent, community-led action to contain the ongoing epidemic of Bundibugyo virus disease. This follows a second meeting of the IHR Emergency Committee on the topic, held on 20 August 2026. The Bundibugyo virus is a member of the Ebola virus family, and the outbreak is currently being managed with temporary recommendations. While no specific case or death counts are provided in the evidence, the WHO has emphasized the need for immediate containment measures. The response includes vaccine distribution and collaboration with local communities to address the health emergency. The situation remains under investigation, and further actions may be required as the outbreak continues to be monitored.

View five news elements
Time
The second meeting of the IHR Emergency Committee on the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo was held on 20 August 2026, with related news releases and statements published on 6 August 2026, 31 July 2026, and 24 August 2026.
Location and population
The event is centered on the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically focusing on the Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak. The affected population includes individuals in the DRC who are at risk of or have been exposed to the Bundibugyo virus.
Event
The WHO and Africa CDC welcomed the allocation of Ebola vaccines to the DRC and called for urgent, community-led action to contain the Ebola outbreak. This is a statement regarding the ongoing epidemic of Bundibugyo virus disease in the DRC, confirmed by the WHO.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak involves the Bundibugyo virus, which is a type of Ebola virus. The WHO has issued temporary recommendations to manage the epidemic, but no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are provided in the evidence. The risk is described as requiring urgent containment measures.
Response, status and uncertainty
The WHO and Africa CDC are actively involved in the response, including vaccine allocation and calling for community-led actions. The recommendations are temporary, indicating that the situation is still under investigation and that further actions may be required.

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

刚果民主共和国与乌干达境内的扎伊尔型埃博拉病毒病疫情 - 欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)

Ebola disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda - European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC)
新闻简报欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)报告刚果民主共和国(DRC)与乌干达境内发生大规模扎伊尔型埃博拉病毒病疫情。该疫情仍在持续,最新更新发布于2026年8月21日。尽管未提供具体病例数与死亡数,但ECDC强调,欧盟/欧洲经济区(EU/EEA)民众的感染风险极低。该机构正积极评估形势,并为各国应对此类潜在疫情提供指导。本次疫情是更广泛传染病关注范围的一部分,包括因持续热浪而增加的蚊媒传播威胁。ECDC收集、分析并分享50余种传染病主题的数据,包括埃博拉相关病毒,以支持欧洲公共卫生应对。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该疫情仍在持续,最新更新发布于2026年8月21日。事件日期未在证据中明确说明,但疫情被描述为刚果民主共和国与乌干达境内的大规模疫情。
地点与对象
疫情发生于刚果民主共和国(DRC)与乌干达。受影响人群包括上述地区民众,但证据中未提供具体人口统计学详情。
事件
刚果民主共和国与乌干达境内正在发生大规模扎伊尔型埃博拉病毒病疫情。该事件由欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)报告,并被确认为持续中的疫情。
规模、影响与风险
疫情被描述为大规模,但证据中未提供具体病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。对欧盟/欧洲经济区(EU/EEA)民众的风险评估为极低。
应对、状态与不确定性
ECDC正积极评估欧洲风险并为各国提供指导,以预防及应对此次疫情。信息仍有限,疫情状态处于调查中。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Ebola disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda - European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC)

News Brief
The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) has reported a large outbreak of Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. The outbreak is ongoing, with the latest updates published on 21 August 2026. While specific case and death counts are not provided, the ECDC emphasizes that the risk of infection for people in the EU/EEA is very low. The agency is actively assessing the situation and providing guidance to help countries prepare for and respond to potential outbreaks. The outbreak is part of a broader focus on infectious diseases, including mosquito-borne threats, which are increasing due to sustained heatwaves. The ECDC collects, analyzes, and shares data on more than 50 infectious disease topics, including Ebola-related viruses, to support public health responses across Europe.
Original source text (English)

Time
The outbreak is ongoing, with the latest updates published on 21 August 2026. The event date is not explicitly stated in the evidence, but the outbreak is described as a large outbreak in the DRC and Uganda.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. The affected population includes people in these regions, though specific demographic details are not provided in the evidence.
Event
A large outbreak of Bundibugyo virus disease is taking place in the DRC and Uganda. The event is reported by the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC), and it is confirmed as an ongoing outbreak.
Scale, impact and risk

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

国际移民组织:多国布尼迪布焦病毒 埃博拉病毒病 暴发 - 第14期疫情报告(2026年8月10日至16日)- 救济网

IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 14 (10-16 August 2026) - ReliefWeb
新闻简报国际移民组织(IOM)第14期疫情报告(2026年8月10日至16日)指出,刚果民主共和国(DRC)境内的布尼迪布焦病毒 暴发 疫情正在迅速升级,已成为该国规模最大且增长最快的疫情。截至8月16日,DRC报告确诊病例5,001例,死亡2,378例,其中社区死亡占总死亡人数的50%以上。该 暴发 已扩散至下韦莱省(Bas-Uélé),引发对向邻国(如南苏丹和中非共和国)跨境 传播 的担忧。IOM已扩大应对规模,重点加强关键入境点与控制点的监测,特别是沿刚果河一带。该 暴发 发生于东部DRC持续三重危机背景下,涉及健康风险、流离失所与冲突。下韦莱省的接触者追踪仍薄弱,需采取跨部门方法以满足流离失所人群的多重需求。报告强调持续开展疾病 监测 与加强区域协调的重要性,以减轻健康风险并确保及时预防与应对。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至2026年8月16日,该 暴发 报告显示疫情正在快速升级。疫情报告发布于2026年8月21日。
地点与对象
受影响地区包括刚果民主共和国(DRC,特别是下韦莱省)及邻国(如乌干达、布隆迪、卢旺达、南苏丹与中非共和国)。该 暴发 涉及DRC境内流离失所者(IDP)营地及上述地区的人群。
事件
世界卫生组织(WHO)确认刚果民主共和国境内的布尼迪布焦病毒 暴发 为该国规模最大且增长最快的 暴发。该事件由国际移民组织(IOM)在其第14期疫情报告中报告。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年8月16日,DRC报告由布尼迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉病毒病确诊病例5,001例,死亡2,378例。社区死亡占总死亡人数的50%以上,表明病例发现与就医存在缺口。该 暴发 已扩散至下韦莱省,且存在向邻国跨境 传播 的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
IOM已扩大应对规模,重点加强入境点(PoEs)与控制点(PoCs)的监测,特别是沿刚果河一带。该 暴发 发生于东部DRC持续三重危机背景下,涉及健康风险、流离失所与冲突。下韦莱省的接触者追踪薄弱,局势需采取跨部门应对措施以满足流离失所人群的多维需求。
A high priorityPublic-health news

IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 14 (10-16 August 2026) - ReliefWeb

News Brief
The IOM Situation Report No. 14 (10-16 August 2026) highlights a rapidly escalating Bundibugyo virus outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), which has become the largest and fastest-growing in the country. As of 16 August, the DRC reported 5,001 confirmed cases and 2,378 deaths, with community deaths accounting for over 50% of fatalities. The outbreak has spread to Bas-Uélé Province, raising concerns about cross-border transmission to neighboring countries like South Sudan and the Central African Republic (CAR). IOM has scaled up its response, focusing on enhancing monitoring at key points of entry and control, particularly along the Congo River. The outbreak is occurring amid an ongoing triple crisis in eastern DRC, involving health risks, displacement, and conflict. Contact tracing remains weak in Bas-Uélé, and a multisectoral approach is needed to address the needs of displaced populations. The report underscores the importance of continued disease surveillance and regional coordination to mitigate health risks and ensure timely prevention and response.
Original source text (English)

Search|t Updates Countries Disasters Organizations Topics Jobs Training Informing humanitarians worldwide 24/7 — a service provided by UN OCHA DR Congo + 9 more IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 14 (10-16 August 2026) Format Situation Report Source IOM Posted 21 Aug 2026 Originally published 21 Aug 2026 Origin View original Attachments Download Report (PDF | 1.38 MB) SITUATION OVERVIEW According to the World Health Organization, as of 16 August, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) reported 5,001 confirmed cases of Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus (BVD), including 2,378 deaths. With the recent increase in figures, the Ebola Bundibugyo outbreak has become the largest and fastest-growing outbreak in DRC. The rapidly escalating outbreak is unfolding against the backdrop of an ongoing triple crisis in eastern DRC, where health risks, largescale displacement and persistent conflict intersect. Given the high case fatality rate of 81% in internally displaced persons (IDP) sites, as per WHO and Africa CDC, a multisectoral response to multidimensional needs of displaced populations is critically required. The spread of the outbreak into Bas-Uélé Province underscores the continued need to comprehensively address the mobility dimensions of the ongoing public health response. Community deaths account for over 50 per cent of fatalities as per government sources, indicating ongoing gaps in case detection and health-care seeking. Contact tracing is particularly weak in Bas-Uélé (18.9%), highlighting the need for continued disease surveillance and monitoring to ensure a complete epidemiological picture. The rapid geographic expansion of the outbreak creates additional needs for hygiene supplies, infection prevention and control (IPC) consumables and health infrastructure, such as ambulances, to support timely prevention, detection, referral and response. In parallel, the rising number of cases in Haut-Uélé requires enhanced disease surveillance and cross-border coordination to prevent disease transmission into neighbouring South Sudan and the Central African Republic (CAR). Cross-border movements, displacement and population mobility continue to shape disease transmission, requiring an urgent and rapid response to save lives and protect people in affected areas, while mitigating regional health risks. Given the rapidly escalating crisis, IOM’s preparedness and response efforts aim to enhance monitoring at points of entry (PoEs) and points of control (PoCs), particularly along the Congo River, to strengthen detection and response capacities. In line with the Inter-Agency Standing Committee (IASC) systemwide scale-up activation for infectious disease events, IOM has scaled up its response, enabling enhanced surge support, operational capacity and coordination across affected countries. IOM’s response continues to focus on Priority 1a countries, DRC and Uganda, where outbreak response and enhanced preparedness activities remain ongoing, and Priority 1b countries, Burundi, Rwanda and South Sudan, which face an elevated risk of crossborder transmission. Preparedness interventions are also being implemented in other at-risk countries across the region, including Priority 2 countries, Angola, CAR, Ethiopia, Kenya, the Republic of the Congo, the United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia, to strengthen readiness and enhance regional health security, while supporting continent-wide efforts, particularly as co-lead of the PoE technical working group at the continental level. Related Content DR Congo + 10 more IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 13 (3-9 August 2026) DR Congo + 10 more IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 12 (27 July - 2 August 2026) DR Congo + 2 more Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak: Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 12, Data as of 02 August 2026 DR Congo + 1 more UNFPA Regional Situation Report - Ebola Bundibugyo Virus Disease Outbreak (1 - 30 June 2026)
View five news elements
Time
As of 16 August 2026, the outbreak was reported to be rapidly escalating. The situation report was published on 21 August 2026.
Location and population
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), particularly Bas-Uélé Province, and neighboring countries such as Uganda, Burundi, Rwanda, South Sudan, and the Central African Republic (CAR) are affected. The outbreak involves internally displaced persons (IDP) sites and populations in these regions.
Event
The Bundibugyo virus outbreak in the DRC is confirmed by the World Health Organization (WHO) as the largest and fastest-growing outbreak in the country. The event is reported by the International Organization for Migration (IOM) in its Situation Report No.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 16 August 2026, the DRC reported 5,001 confirmed cases of Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus, with 2,378 deaths. Community deaths account for over 50% of fatalities, indicating gaps in case detection and healthcare seeking. The outbreak has spread to Bas-Uélé Province, and there is a risk of transmission to neighboring countries.
Response, status and uncertainty
IOM has scaled up its response, focusing on enhancing monitoring at points of entry (PoEs) and points of control (PoCs), particularly along the Congo River. The outbreak is unfolding against a backdrop of an ongoing triple crisis in eastern DRC, including health risks, displacement, and conflict. Contact tracing is weak in Bas-Uélé, and the situation requires a multisectoral response to address the multidimensional needs of displaced populations.

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

WHO非洲区域办事处|刚果民主共和国|乌干达第15期埃博拉病毒病(布尼迪布乔病毒)每周外部形势报告,截至2026年8月23日数据

EBOLA BUNDIBUGYO VIRUS DISEASE OUTBREAK Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 15, Data as of 23 August 2026 - WHO | Regional Office for Africa
新闻简报世界卫生组织非洲区域办事处于2026年8月23日发布的报告显示,刚果民主共和国正在暴发埃博拉病毒病(布尼迪布乔病毒),该国自疫情被宣布以来已记录超过5200例病例,并已进入第100天。报告同时指出乌干达在终结埃博拉病毒病方面取得进展。本次疫情由布尼迪布乔病毒引起,世卫组织正积极参与区域应对工作,包括跨境磋商与加强准备。疫情仍在调查中,未提供具体死亡人数。报告强调需继续采取遏制与应对措施,以减轻疾病传播与影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
报告发布日期为2026年8月23日,涉及事件日期为2026年8月15日至2026年8月24日。
地点与对象
本次埃博拉病毒病(布尼迪布乔病毒)疫情报告于刚果民主共和国与乌干达发生,重点关注布尼迪布乔病毒病。
事件
报告详述刚果民主共和国的埃博拉病毒病疫情,指出该国自疫情被宣布以来已记录超过5200例病例,并已进入第100天。报告同时提及乌干达正在倒计时终结埃博拉病毒病。
规模、影响与风险
刚果民主共和国已记录超过5200例病例,未提供具体死亡人数。报告强调疫情仍在持续,并需加强区域准备。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织正积极应对本次疫情,开展区域审查与跨境磋商。疫情状态仍在调查中,报告强调需继续加强遏制与应对工作。
A high priorityPublic-health news

EBOLA BUNDIBUGYO VIRUS DISEASE OUTBREAK Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 15, Data as of 23 August 2026 - WHO | Regional Office for Africa

News Brief
The report from the WHO Regional Office for Africa, dated 23 August 2026, highlights an ongoing Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, which has recorded over 5200 cases as it marks 100 days since the outbreak was declared. The report also notes Uganda's progress in ending the outbreak. The outbreak is attributed to the Bundibugyo virus, and the WHO is actively involved in regional response efforts, including cross-border consultations and strengthening preparedness. The situation remains under investigation, with no specific death count provided. The report underscores the importance of continued containment and response measures to mitigate the spread and impact of the disease.
Original source text (English)

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

埃博拉 暴发:现状 - 疾病控制与预防中心 |疾病预防控制中心 (.gov)

Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)
新闻简报美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)正在应对由本迪布焦病毒(Bundibugyo virus)引发的埃博拉病毒病(Ebola 暴发)暴发,该暴发发生在刚果民主共和国(DRC)和乌干达的偏远地区。此次暴发始于2026年8月,是有记录以来第二大暴发,在响应行动启动后40天内确诊病例已超过1,000例。美国尚未确认任何病例,且疾病传入美国的风险被认为非常低。此次暴发的传播速度较以往更快,在DRC多个省份报告了病例,包括上乌埃莱省、伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省和特舒波省。在乌干达,所有病例均在坎帕拉市确诊,未报告社区传播。由于医疗基础设施有限、冲突持续和公众信任缺失,响应行动面临挑战。为降低风险,美国已暂时限制来自DRC、乌干达和南苏丹的旅客入境,并要求实施21天隔离。建议前往疫区的旅行者采取加强防护措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年8月25日,截至2026-08-24。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国(DRC)和乌干达,DRC特别提及伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省和特舒波省,乌干达特别提及坎帕拉市。
事件
CDC正在应对由本迪布焦病毒引发的埃博拉病毒病暴发,暴发发生在DRC和乌干达的偏远地区。此次暴发的传播速度较以往显著加快,目前已成为有记录以来第二大暴发。
规模、影响与风险
此次暴发在响应行动启动后40天内确诊病例已超过1,000例,死亡病例数迅速上升。美国尚未确认任何病例,且疾病传入美国的风险被认为非常低。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC已对来自DRC、乌干达和南苏丹的旅客实施临时入境限制,并要求实施21天隔离。由于医疗基础设施有限、冲突持续和公众对政府缺乏信任,响应行动面临挑战。建议前往疫区的旅行者采取加强防护措施。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)

News Brief
The CDC is addressing an Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo virus in remote regions of the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. This outbreak, which began in August 2026, is the second-largest on record and has surpassed 1,000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation. No cases have been confirmed in the United States, and the risk of spreading to the U.S. is considered very low. The outbreak is spreading faster than previous ones, with cases reported in several DRC provinces, including Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu, Sud-Kivu, and Tshopo. In Uganda, all cases have been diagnosed in Kampala, with no community spread reported. The response faces challenges due to limited healthcare infrastructure, ongoing conflict, and lack of public trust. To mitigate risks, the U.S. has temporarily restricted entry for travelers from DRC, Uganda, and South Sudan, requiring a 21-day quarantine period. Enhanced precautions are advised for those traveling to affected areas.

Español Key points CDC is responding to an outbreak of Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus in remote areas of the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. To date, no cases of Ebola disease have been confirmed in the United States because of this outbreak. The overall risk to the American public and travelers remains low. Outbreak overview Risk to the United States To date, no Ebola cases associated with this outbreak have been reported in the United States. The likelihood of Ebola spreading to the United States is considered very low. If a case was diagnosed in the United States, the risk of Ebola spreading in the United States is also low due to the strength of our public health system and infection control measures. CDC is responding to an outbreak of Ebola in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. This outbreak is spreading substantially faster than previous Ebola outbreaks and is now the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record. The outbreak is caused by Bundibugyo virus, a type of Ebola virus, in northeastern DRC and Uganda's capital Kampala. In DRC, cases have been confirmed in Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu (North Kivu), Sud-Kivu (South Kivu), and Tshopo provinces. This is the 17th outbreak of Ebola in DRC. This Ebola outbreak surpassed 1,000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation. In comparison, the 2018 Ebola outbreak in DRC took approximately 235 days to reach more than 1,000 cases. South Kivu has reported no confirmed cases since May 29. The number of reported deaths has risen rapidly as well. All cases in Uganda so far have been diagnosed in its capital of Kampala, with no community spread reported. Uganda's last reported case, confirmed on June 21, was related to travel from DRC. The response to this outbreak is challenging due to a number of complex circumstances in the region. Limited health infrastructure, including limited access to healthcare, diagnostic and laboratory services, infection prevention and control supplies, and other basic services make stopping this outbreak more difficult. Ongoing conflict in the area where the outbreak is occurring creates security issues and complicates contact tracing and infection prevention and control. Violence against healthcare workers and shortages of personal protective equipment are leading to healthcare workers becoming infected while treating patients. Frequent population movement and cross-border travel increase the risk of spread to Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda, and to other areas. Lack of trust in the government and misinformation make communicating about Ebola more difficult. To reduce the risk of Ebola importation into the United States, U.S. entry for travelers who were recently in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is temporarily restricted. Travelers, including Americans, who have been in DRC within 21 days of their flight will not be allowed to board commercial flights with U.S. destinations. All U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in DRC should plan to remain outside DRC for 21 days before entering the United States. This includes all passengers with an intermediate stop in DRC or on multi-stop itineraries via DRC regardless of whether they disembark or stay on the airplane. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in Uganda or South Sudan (and not present in DRC) within 21 days of arrival in the United States must only enter through designated airports for enhanced screening. This process is in addition to CDC's renewed August 12 order, under 42 CFR 71.40, that continued the temporary entry restrictions to the United States for certain categories of travelers after they were in DRC, Uganda, or South Sudan. CDC recommends avoiding all travel to Ituri and Nord-Kivu (North Kivu) provinces and avoiding nonessential travel to Haut-Uele and Tshopo provinces in DRC. Travelers to other parts of DRC or Uganda should take enhanced precautions to avoid Ebola exposure and monitor for symptoms while traveling and for 21 days after leaving. Latest data Reported cases This is a rapidly evolving situation, and case counts are subject to change. The DRC MoH does not include probable cases in their official reporting. Suspected cases and suspected deaths in DRC have been temporarily excluded from the count pending ongoing investigations and data standardization. The Uganda MoH does not include suspected cases in their official reporting. Map of affected areas View LargerDownload To date, the Ebola disease outbreak in DRC has been confirmed in Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu (North Kivu), Sud-Kivu (South Kivu), and Tshopo provinces. Cases related to the DRC outbreak also have been reported in Uganda's capital of Kampala. CDC response CDC is working internationally and domestically to respond to this outbreak and prevent Ebola from entering the United States. About 500 people at CDC are involved in responding to the outbreak, including more than 120 people deployed to the affected countries. 新研究将检验现有埃博拉病毒疫苗是否能对邦迪布焦埃博拉病毒产生交叉保护 - CEPI

New studies to test whether existing Ebola vaccines can generate immune responses to Bundibugyo ebolavirus - CEPI
新闻简报流行病防范创新联盟(CEPI)正在开展新研究,以确定现有埃博拉病毒疫苗是否能对邦迪布焦埃博拉病毒产生交叉保护。该研究是CEPI更广泛战略的一部分,旨在通过加速疫苗及其他生物制剂对策的研发,为应对未来大流行做准备。研究旨在评估当前疫苗对该病毒株的有效性。目前证据中未报告具体病例数、死亡数或暴露人数,但该研究是全球减少流行病和大流行威胁风险的一项努力。CEPI的方法包括财政投入、合作伙伴关系及倡导,以确保疫苗及工具的公平可及性。证据未说明当前调查或检测的具体状态,但强调了应对新发传染病的持续努力。

时间
桌面端导航加载中。
地点与对象
移动端导航加载中。
事件
流行病防范创新联盟(CEPI)正在开展新研究,以检验现有埃博拉病毒疫苗是否能对邦迪布焦埃博拉病毒产生交叉保护。该事件由CEPI(流行病防范创新联盟)报告。
规模、影响与风险
研究旨在评估现有疫苗对邦迪布焦埃博拉病毒的有效性,该病毒是一种特定类型的埃博拉病毒。目前证据中未报告具体病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。风险未明确说明,但该研究是更广泛应对未来大流行威胁努力的一部分。
应对、状态与不确定性
CEPI正在积极推进疫苗研发及针对流行病和大流行威胁的对策。该研究是其加速疫苗研发战略的一部分。证据未说明当前调查或检测的具体状态,但表明相关工作正在持续进行。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

New studies to test whether existing Ebola vaccines can generate immune responses to Bundibugyo ebolavirus - CEPI

News Brief
CEPI, the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, is conducting new studies to determine whether existing Ebola vaccines can generate immune responses to the Bundibugyo ebolavirus. This research is part of CEPI's broader strategy to prepare for future pandemics by accelerating the development of vaccines and other biologic countermeasures. The studies aim to evaluate the effectiveness of current vaccines against this specific strain of the virus. While no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported in the evidence, the research is part of a global effort to reduce the risk of epidemic and pandemic threats. CEPI's approach includes financial investments, partnerships, and advocacy to ensure equitable access to vaccines and tools. The evidence does not specify the current status of the investigation or testing, but it highlights ongoing efforts to address emerging infectious diseases.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

刚果押注‘旧’疫苗应对新型疫情,而新型疫情正在扩散

Congo gambles on ‘old’ vaccine as new Ebola virus spirals
新闻简报In the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), health officials are using the Ervebo vaccine, originally developed for the Zaire strain of Ebola, to combat a rapidly spreading Bundibugyo virus outbreak in the Ituri province. The outbreak, which began in mid-May, has already claimed around 2,500 lives and is doubling every 20 days, potentially becoming the largest ever recorded by the end of September. While the World Health Organization (WHO) has allocated 20,000 doses for a Phase 3 clinical trial to assess the vaccine's effectiveness against the Bundibugyo strain, the remaining 50,000 doses are intended for front-line health workers, who are at high risk of infection due to exposure to contagious bodily fluids. Early laboratory and animal data suggest Ervebo may provide some protection, but its efficacy against the Bundibugyo virus remains unconfirmed. Health workers who were vaccinated during a previous Zaire strain outbreak in 2018-2020 reportedly did not contract the disease during the current Bundibugyo outbreak, though this is anecdotal. The WHO emphasizes that while the vaccine may not stop transmission, it could save lives and reduce suffering during the development of a specific vaccine for the Bundibugyo strain.

时间
本迪布焦病毒疫情每20天病例数量翻一番,预计到9月底将成为史上规模最大的此类疫情。Ervebo疫苗的调拨公布于本周早些时候,而本迪布焦病毒疫情于5月中旬被宣布。
地点与对象
本迪布焦病毒疫情主要集中在刚果民主共和国伊图里省。受影响人群包括一线医护人员及刚果东北部地区的普通民众。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo gambles on ‘old’ vaccine as new Ebola virus spirals

News Brief
In the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), health officials are using the Ervebo vaccine, originally developed for the Zaire strain of Ebola, to combat a rapidly spreading Bundibugyo virus outbreak in the Ituri province. The outbreak, which began in mid-May, has already claimed around 2,500 lives and is doubling every 20 days, potentially becoming the largest ever recorded by the end of September. While the World Health Organization (WHO) has allocated 20,000 doses for a Phase 3 clinical trial to assess the vaccine's effectiveness against the Bundibugyo strain, the remaining 50,000 doses are intended for front-line health workers, who are at high risk of infection due to exposure to contagious bodily fluids. Early laboratory and animal data suggest Ervebo may provide some protection, but its efficacy against the Bundibugyo virus remains unconfirmed. Health workers who were vaccinated during a previous Zaire strain outbreak in 2018-2020 reportedly did not contract the disease during the current Bundibugyo outbreak, though this is anecdotal. The WHO emphasizes that while the vaccine may not stop transmission, it could save lives and reduce suffering during the development of a specific vaccine for the Bundibugyo strain.
Original source text (English)
Congo is to try using an existing vaccine against its spiralling Ebola outbreak, even though the jab was developed to protect against a different strain of the virus. There is currently no specific vaccine to protect against the Bundibugyo, the strain of Ebola sweeping the north East of the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). However, it has been given 70,000 doses of the Ervebo vaccine, a jab that has proven effective against the Zaire strain of the disease. Scientists hope it will provide at least some protection in the fastest ever outbreak of the deadly haemorrhagic fever while a new vaccine is developed. The outbreak has killed around 2,500 people, is doubling every 20 days , and is on track to be the largest ever known by around the end of September. The outbreak has so far killed 2,500 people and is doubling every 20 days - Luke Dray/Getty Images Europe Nearly half of all those infected have gone on to die. Dr Sania Nishtar, chief executive of the Gavi global vaccine partnership, earlier this week said to beat the outbreak "we must deploy every tool at our disposal". She went on: "To avoid this becoming the worst recorded Ebola outbreak in history, it is critical that all effective countermeasure options are brought to bear. "We have a tool that could help save lives while we wait for [Bundibugyo]-specific vaccines to become available." Previous clinical trials have shown that Ervebo is safe, but its effect on other strains is unknown. The World Health Organisation said in a statement that while it was not known if Ervebo may protect against the Bundibugyo virus, "early laboratory and animal data suggest it may provide some protection". Medics told the Telegraph of anecdotal claims that health workers who had been given Ervebo to protect them during an outbreak of the Zaire strain in 2018 to 2020 had not fallen ill during the current Bundibugyo outbreak. Health workers are at extreme risk of catching the disease because they come into contact with highly-contagious blood, vomit and diarrhoea from dying patients. When they fall sick themselves, they frequently go on to infect their own families. Giving health workers vaccine jabs to protect them as they tend the sick is a cornerstone of Ebola responses. The WHO said the vaccine allocation includes 20,000 doses for a Phase 3 clinical trial to understand the impact of the vaccine on the Bundibugyo virus. The remaining 50,000 doses will be used for front-line and health workers as recommended by WHO experts. Dr Nishtar said: "Having already been licensed for the Zaire strain, we know that Ervebo is proven to be safe. "And while the signs are that it may not stop transmission – which is essential for ending the outbreak - it could well save precious lives and reduce suffering." Health officials warn that the Bundibugyo outbreak centred on DRC's Ituri province is easily outpacing relief efforts . The virus is thought to have been spreading for months before an outbreak was finally declared in mid-May and medics have been unable to make up the head start. An analysis by the Africa Centres for Disease Control meanwhile warned the current outbreak dwarfed all others on record The current outbreak had almost ten times more cases than any other previous outbreak at a similar stage. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

CEPI commits $4.17m to study vaccine protection against Ebola virus

CEPI commits $4.17m to study vaccine protection against Ebola virus
新闻简报CEPI has committed $4.17 million to study whether existing Ebola vaccines can offer protection against the Bundibugyo virus, which is currently causing a significant outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The outbreak, declared a Public Health Emergency of International Concern in May, has resulted in 4,665 confirmed cases and 2,184 deaths across five provinces and 54 health zones. The research will examine blood samples from over 1,700 people who received the Ervebo vaccine and another Ebola vaccine. CEPI’s initiative aims to determine the effectiveness of these vaccines against the Bundibugyo virus, which does not yet have a licensed vaccine. The DRC authorities and Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention have also announced plans to deploy Ervebo to priority groups under an expanded-use protocol. This study is crucial for informing vaccine strategies during the ongoing outbreak.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究正值刚果民主共和国(DRC)继续应对由布尼迪布焦埃博拉病毒(N1、N6)引发的暴发疫情之际。本文发布日期为 2026 年 8 月 21 日(N2)。
地点与对象
该暴发疫情正在刚果民主共和国(DRC)境内发生,具体涉及五个省份及 54 个卫生区(N9)。
事件
CEPI has committed $4.17 million to study whether existing Ebola vaccines can generate immune responses against the Bundibugyo virus (N1, N2). The event is confirmed based on the news release and official statements (N1, N2).
规模、影响与风险
刚果民主共和国暴发疫情已累计报告 4,665 例确诊病例和 2,184 例死亡(N9)。该暴发疫情已于 5 月被宣布为国际关注的突发公共卫生事件(N10)。研究将检测超过 1,700 名接种过 Ervebo 及另一种埃博拉疫苗的人员的血液样本(N25)。
应对、状态与不确定性
CEPI 支持的研究正在进行,以分析疫苗接种者储存的血液样本(N4、N25)。刚果民主共和国当局及非洲疾病预防控制中心已宣布计划在方案指导下的扩展使用安排中向重点人群部署 Ervebo(N16)。

CEPI commits $4.17m to study vaccine protection against Ebola virus

News Brief
CEPI has committed $4.17 million to study whether existing Ebola vaccines can offer protection against the Bundibugyo virus, which is currently causing a significant outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The outbreak, declared a Public Health Emergency of International Concern in May, has resulted in 4,665 confirmed cases and 2,184 deaths across five provinces and 54 health zones. The research will examine blood samples from over 1,700 people who received the Ervebo vaccine and another Ebola vaccine. CEPI’s initiative aims to determine the effectiveness of these vaccines against the Bundibugyo virus, which does not yet have a licensed vaccine. The DRC authorities and Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention have also announced plans to deploy Ervebo to priority groups under an expanded-use protocol. This study is crucial for informing vaccine strategies during the ongoing outbreak.
Original source text (English)

Governorship Election Presidential Gubernatorial Picture illustrating Ebola CEPI commits $4.17m to study vaccine protection against Ebola virus The research comes as the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continues to battle the outbreak caused by Bundibugyo ebolavirus. byMariam Ileyemi August 21, 2026 Reading Time: 3 mins read 0 Add us on Google The Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI) has committed $4.17 million to four research projects examining whether existing Ebola vaccines can generate immune responses against the Bundibugyo virus. Join the Premium Times WhatsApp Community For Quick Access To News and Happenings Around You. Open in WhatsApp The studies, according to a news release on the CEPI website, will analyse stored blood samples from thousands of people previously vaccinated against Zaire ebolavirus, including recipients of the Ervebo vaccine. This will help to determine whether the vaccines could offer some protection against the different Ebola virus species. The research comes as the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continues to battle the outbreak caused by Bundibugyo ebolavirus. Stay Ahead with Premium Times Follow us on Google News and never miss breaking stories, investigations, and in-depth reporting. Add as a preferred source on Google The World Health Organisation (WHO) on Wednesday declared the ongoing outbreak in the country the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record. According to WHO’s latest disease outbreak update, 4,665 confirmed cases and 2,184 deaths have been recorded across five provinces and 54 health zones. WHO also declared the outbreak a Public Health Emergency of International Concern in May. No licensed Bundibugyo vaccine According to the CEO of CEPI, Richard Hatchett, there is currently no licensed vaccine specifically approved for Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease. Hatchett said Bundibugyo-specific vaccine candidates are being developed but would not be available during the earliest phase of the current outbreak. “However, vaccines do exist for the related Zaire ebolavirus, including Ervebo. Given the recent announcement that Ervebo will be deployed for emergency use against Bundibugyo virus, these CEPI-backed studies will be all the more important to inform on how we can make the best use of the existing vaccine,” he said. CEPI noted that the WHO Technical Advisory Group on Candidate Vaccine Prioritisation has recommended that Ervebo be evaluated in a phase three clinical trial alongside Bundibugyo-specific vaccine candidates when they become available. It added that the DRC authorities and the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention have also announced plans to deploy Ervebo to priority groups under a protocol-governed expanded-use arrangement. Research to guide vaccine decisions CEPI said the projects were selected through its June 2026 call for proposals aimed at generating evidence for Bundibugyo vaccine research and development. The organisation said the studies would help address gaps in knowledge about the potential use of existing vaccines against the virus and support decisions on vaccination during the current outbreak and future responses. The funding is supported by the European Commission through a 2026 Horizon Europe grant, the United States Department of State and CEPI’s “existing core funding.” What the studies will examine CEPI said the four projects will focus on understanding whether vaccination against Zaire ebolavirus can trigger antibody responses that recognise Bundibugyo ebolavirus. The studies will also help establish and standardise antibody tests for Bundibugyo virus and examine how long such immune responses last. “Researchers will undertake antibody testing against all Orthoebolavirus species and Marburg virus, prioritising tests for Bundibugyo ebolavirus. They will examine which vaccines potentially provide cross-protection against Bundibugyo virus and identify vaccination regimens that might be associated with broad and durable cross-reactive immune responses,” CEPI noted. “Part of the award will transfer these laboratory methods to INRB in DRC, harmonising their laboratory capacity with that of CEPI’s Centralised Laboratory Network and international antibody reference standards. First antibody results are expected within two to six months.” Institutions involved CEPI added that the University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) Fielding School of Public Health will receive $2.05 million for its study. The researchers will examine blood samples from more than 1,700 people who received Ervebo and another Ebola vaccine. The Institute of Tropical Medicine in Antwerp will receive $1.02 million for its EBO-BOOST-BRIDGE project, which will study samples from more than 600 participants. READ ALSO: WHO raises Ebola risk level as DRC outbreak worsens, reports new hantavirus cases CEPI said the project would also transfer laboratory methods to the National Institute of Biomedical Research in the DRC, with the first results expected within two to six months. The University of Oxford’s Nuffield Department of Medicine’s Centre for Human Genetics and Pandemic Sciences Institute will receive $975,000 to study blood samples from people who received Ervebo and survivors of Zaire ebolavirus disease. The study will examine whether immunity from vaccination or previous infection could potentially protect against Bundibugyo virus. The MRC-University of Glasgow Centre for Virus Research will receive $120,000 to study samples from vaccinated close contacts and Ugandan healthcare workers. The researchers will determine whether antibodies generated by Ervebo also recognise Bundibugyo virus. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

CEPI 资助研究:针对埃博拉病毒疫苗对布迪布焦病毒的保护作用——《商业线》

CEPI funds studies on Ebola vaccine protection against Bundibugyo virus - BusinessLine
新闻简报流行病防范创新联盟(CEPI)正在资助四个研究项目,以评估现有针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒的疫苗(如 Ervebo)是否能为布迪布焦埃博拉病毒提供保护。这些研究旨在表征先前接种过扎伊尔埃博拉病毒疫苗的个体中的免疫应答,并评估抗体对布迪布焦病毒的交叉反应性。该研究将帮助建立布迪布焦病毒特异性抗体检测,并界定免疫应答的持久性。CEPI 表示,尽管目前尚无针对布迪布焦病毒的获批疫苗,现有疫苗或可在该病毒暴发的早期阶段发挥作用。这些研究是更广泛努力的一部分,旨在为该病毒开发疫苗,该病毒已与刚果民主共和国迅速升级的暴发相关。研究结果将为未来疫苗接种策略及广谱保护性丝状病毒疫苗的评估提供依据。
查看新闻五要素
时间
这些研究于 2026 年进行,世卫组织于 2026 年 5 月宣布该疫情为国际关注的突发公共卫生事件(N10)。文章发表于 2026 年 8 月 20 日(N6)。
地点与对象
研究聚焦布迪布焦埃博拉病毒,该病毒与刚果民主共和国的暴发相关(N8)。研究涉及先前接种过扎伊尔埃博拉病毒疫苗的个体,包括以往疫苗接种活动和临床试验中的参与者(N16)。
事件
CEPI 正在资助四个研究项目,以评估针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒获批的疫苗(如 Ervebo)是否能为布迪布焦埃博拉病毒提供保护(N7)。这些研究旨在表征免疫应答并评估抗体的交叉反应性(N17)。
规模、影响与风险
这些研究是为应对缺乏针对布迪布焦病毒获批疫苗的努力的一部分(N12)。CEPI 正在资助多个布迪布焦病毒疫苗候选产品,但现有针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒的疫苗可能无法在该暴发的最早阶段使用(N13)。研究结果将帮助界定未来疫苗接种策略,并评估广谱保护性丝状病毒疫苗(N19)。
应对、状态与不确定性
CEPI is supporting the studies with $4.17 million, and the research teams will use stored blood samples to analyse immune responses (N9, N16). The findings will aid in interpreting immune responses in clinical trials and defining the dynamics of antibody responses (N18, N19). The studies are ongoing and will provide critical data for future strategies (N19).
B routine priorityPublic-health news

CEPI funds studies on Ebola vaccine protection against Bundibugyo virus - BusinessLine

News Brief
CEPI, the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, is funding four research projects to evaluate whether existing vaccines against Zaire ebolavirus, such as Ervebo, could offer protection against the Bundibugyo ebolavirus. These studies aim to characterise immune responses in individuals previously vaccinated against Zaire ebolavirus and assess cross-reactivity of antibodies against Bundibugyo. The research will help establish tests for Bundibugyo-specific antibodies and define the durability of immune responses. CEPI has stated that while no licensed vaccine exists for Bundibugyo, existing vaccines may be useful in the early stages of the outbreak. The studies are part of broader efforts to develop vaccines for this virus, which has been linked to a rapidly escalating outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The findings will inform future vaccination strategies and the evaluation of broadly protective filovirus vaccines.
Original source text (English)

Home News Science CEPI-backed studies to assess Ebola vaccine response against Bundibugyo ebolavirus Researchers will analyse immune responses in previously vaccinated people to determine whether existing Ebola vaccines could offer protection against the related virus. By Our Bureau Updated - August 20, 2026 at 09:20 PM. GIFT THIS ARTICLE This is a premium feature available only to paid subscribers of businessline Subscribe × Share WhatsAppXFacebookLinkedInMessengerRedditMail https://www.thehindubusinessline.com/news/science/cepi-backed-studies-to-assess-ebola-vaccine-response-against-bundibugyo-ebolavirus/article71370328.eceCopy CEPI is funding four research projects to assess whether vaccines licensed against Zaire ebolavirus, including Ervebo, could provide protection against Bundibugyo ebolavirus. New studies are underway to assess if vaccines licensed against the Zaire ebolavirus—including Ervebo—could offer protection against Bundibugyo ebolavirus, the virus behind the rapidly escalating outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The studies are supported by the international organisation CEPI – the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, which is providing $4.17 million to four research projects conducting them. The Ebola outbreak was declared a Public Health Emergency of International Concern by the World Health Organisation (WHO) in May 2026. The four awardees undertaking the study include research groups led by UCLA (USA), the Institute of Tropical Medicine (Antwerp), the University of Oxford (UK), and the MRC-University of Glasgow Centre for Virus Research (UK). “There is no licensed vaccine against Bundibugyo virus. CEPI is funding multiple Bundibugyo vaccine candidates to address this gap, but even on the most compressed timelines those vaccines will not be available for the earliest phase of this outbreak”, said Dr Richard Hatchett, CEPI Chief Executive Officer. “However, vaccines do exist for the related Zaire ebolavirus, including Ervebo. Given the recent announcement that Ervebo will be deployed for emergency use against Bundibugyo virus, these CEPI-backed studies will be all the more important to inform on how we can make the best use of the existing vaccine,” he added. Explaining how it would work, CEPI said research teams will analyse stored blood samples from individuals vaccinated with licensed/previously licensed vaccines against Zaire ebolavirus either in previous vaccination campaigns and/or clinical trials. They aim to characterise vaccine-induced immune responses and assess cross-reactivity of antibody responses against Bundibugyo ebolavirus. The critical data that these serology studies will provide will help establish and standardise tests for antibodies against Bundibugyo virus and use longitudinal samples to define the dynamics and durability of these responses, it explained. Data from these studies will also aid interpretation of immune responses in clinical trials, help assess whether Bundibugyo-specific antibodies are associated with protection, and inform future vaccination strategies and evaluation of Bundibugyo-specific and broadly protective filovirus vaccines, CEPI said. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

在埃博拉病毒病应对持续之际,刚果民主共和国(DRC)的其他健康需求不容忽视

As the Ebola disease response continues, other health needs in DRC must not be neglected
新闻简报本文强调刚果民主共和国(DRC)在应对埃博拉病毒病持续之际面临的挑战。尽管埃博拉病毒病于2026年5月被宣布暴发,但对这一紧急情况的关注并未阻止其他健康危机在东部地区蔓延,特别是在北基伍省和南基伍省。麻疹和霍乱仍在活跃传播,报告疑似麻疹病例108,643例,死亡1,394例;疑似霍乱病例35,464例,死亡1,051例。无国界医生(MSF)正在应对这些疫情,已治疗超过2,410名霍乱患者,并为超过283,150名儿童接种疫苗。然而,文章强调当前应对仍显不足,且对其他健康需求的忽视可能导致可预防的死亡。文章还指出,对埃博拉病毒病的恐惧正阻碍患者寻求其他疾病的医疗服务,进一步加剧局势。MSF呼吁加大支持与资金投入,以确保在多重危机叠加下仍能获得基本医疗服务。
查看新闻五要素
时间
埃博拉病毒病于2026年5月被宣布暴发,本文发布于2026年8月24日。麻疹和霍乱疫情在北基伍省和南基伍省持续。
地点与对象
疫情发生于刚果民主共和国(DRC),具体在北基伍省和南基伍省。受影响人群包括因冲突而流离失所的儿童及家庭。
事件
文章报道称,在埃博拉病毒病应对持续之际,麻疹和霍乱等其他健康需求正在DRC东部地区蔓延。这些健康危机与埃博拉病毒病疫情并存,亟需同时应对。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年7月19日,报告疑似麻疹病例108,643例,麻疹死亡1,394例;疑似霍乱病例35,464例,霍乱死亡1,051例。MSF团队已治疗超过2,410名霍乱患者,并为超过283,150名儿童接种疫苗。文章指出,在应对埃博拉病毒病期间忽视其他健康需求可能导致可预防的死亡。
应对、状态与不确定性
MSF正在北基伍省和南基伍省应对麻疹和霍乱疫情。然而,动员力度仍显不足,亟需卫生当局和人道主义行动者加大承诺。文章指出,资金投入正在下降,而需求持续上升,局势被描述为需要同时应对的重大紧急情况。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As the Ebola disease response continues, other health needs in DRC must not be neglected

News Brief
This article highlights the ongoing challenges in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) as the Ebola disease response continues. While the Ebola outbreak was declared in May 2026, the focus on this emergency has not prevented other health crises from spreading in the eastern regions, particularly in North and South Kivu provinces. Measles and cholera outbreaks are reported to be active, with over 108,643 suspected measles cases and 1,394 deaths, as well as 35,464 suspected cholera cases and 1,051 deaths. Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF) is addressing these outbreaks, having treated over 2,410 cholera patients and vaccinated more than 283,150 children. However, the article emphasizes that the response remains insufficient, and there is a growing concern about the neglect of other health needs, which could lead to preventable deaths. The article also mentions that the fear of Ebola is deterring patients from seeking care for other conditions, further complicating the situation. MSF calls for increased support and funding to ensure access to essential healthcare services amid these overlapping crises.
Original source text (English)

Louange Uwizeye, 29 years old, took her son to the Buhimba medical centre after days of vomiting and diarrhoea, and whose symptoms were similar to cholera. © MSF Since the Ebola disease outbreak was declared in May, media attention and resources have focused largely on the response to this emergency. While this mobilisation is essential to contain the outbreak, it must not come at the expense of other health and humanitarian needs that persist in Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC)'s North and South Kivu provinces. Cholera, measles, malaria, and violence linked to the ongoing conflict continue to threaten thousands of people in North and South Kivu. Médecins Sans Frontières/Doctors Without Borders (MSF) is responding to the measles and cholera outbreaks affecting the two provinces. The Ebola disease outbreak is a major emergency, but it is not the only one... Every day, we receive patients suffering from various diseases, people injured in fighting, and survivors of sexual violence. For these communities, all these emergencies coexist and require a simultaneous response. Joshua Eckley MSF head of programs in South Kivu MSF continues to support health facilities and various medical needs, but additional support is needed. We call on all humanitarian actors to ensure safe access to essential healthcare services in DRC despite the Ebola disease outbreak, in order to avoid a higher risk of preventable deaths. Ebola is making headlines, but it’s not the only crisis in DRC DRC is experiencing multiple simultaneous health and humanitarian crises. Communities are already living under the pressure of armed conflict, while repeated clashes cause displacement, interrupt vaccination campaigns, limit access to safe drinking water, and make healthcare harder to access. Added to this fragile situation is the fear now associated with Ebola disease. Fear of exposure to the virus can deter patients from seeking healthcare, potentially leading to delays in treatment or deaths, including from preventable diseases. “The Ebola disease outbreak is a major emergency, but it is not the only one,” explains Joshua Eckley, MSF head of programs in South Kivu. “Every day, we receive patients suffering from various diseases, people injured in fighting, and survivors of sexual violence. For these communities, all these emergencies coexist and require a simultaneous response. When basic healthcare is interrupted, more people risk dying from preventable diseases. It is essential to preserve continuity of care and access to primary healthcare in order to treat all other conditions.” In the shadow of Ebola, measles and cholera are spreading in the country’s east MSF teams have mobilised six interventions in response to measles outbreaks in North and South Kivu. The most recent are still ongoing in Shabunda, South Kivu, and Walikale, North Kivu. MSF teams have already treated more than 12,050 patients and vaccinated more than 283,150 children. Medical staff take care of a patient at the Buhimba cholera treatment centre in Goma, North Kivu. © Ley Uwera At the same time, we are seeing a worrying resurgence of cholera. In Bukavu and Shabunda, South Kivu, MSF continues to care for patients following a surge in cases recorded last year in the province. In Goma, teams were also mobilised to tackle a cholera outbreak between May and early August 2026. In total, more than 2,410 people with cholera have already been treated in MSF-supported facilities. Measles and cholera in DRC While the Ebola disease outbreak is the focus of attention, other outbreaks are spreading in eastern DRC. As of 19 July there were: 108,643 suspected measles cases (of those, more than 50,827 in North and South Kivu) 1,394 deaths from measles More than 35,464 suspected cholera cases (14,819 in North and South Kivu) More than 1,051 deaths from cholera. “For the families we care for, there is no hierarchy between crises,” says Dr Yvan Mbangi, MSF deputy medical coordinator in Goma. “Whether a child dies of measles or malaria, whether a person succumbs to cholera or Ebola disease, every life matters.” Beyond the response to Ebola disease, measles, and cholera outbreaks, MSF continues to provide essential healthcare in Masisi, Mweso, Walikale, Bambo, Kibirizi, Kirotshe, Goma, and Rutshuru in North Kivu, and in Minova, Bunyakiri, and Uvira in South Kivu. But given the scale of the needs, this mobilisation remains largely inadequate. These crises require a much greater commitment from health authorities and humanitarian actors to guarantee access to care for the most vulnerable people. View of the Ruzizi River and the hills overlooking the city of Bukavu, South Kivu province, where MSF is present and responding to a cholera outbreak. © Ley Uwera Funding is decreasing amid rising needs While humanitarian needs continue to increase, available funding is decreasing. Despite malaria remaining the leading cause of morbidity in DRC, North Kivu could notably be excluded from the next three-year Global Fund funding cycle dedicated to the fight against malaria. “We risk seeing a reduction in the resources dedicated to the disease that kills the most people in this region,” warns Dr Mbangi. “It would be a tragic paradox: mobilising considerable resources against one outbreak while weakening the response to a disease that continues to cause a far higher number of deaths every day.” For the families we care for, there is no hierarchy between crises. Whether a child dies of measles or malaria, whether a person succumbs to cholera or Ebola disease, every life matters. Dr Yvan Mbangi MSF deputy medical coordinator in Goma Years of conflict, massive population displacement and chronic insecurity, as well as the drastic reduction in humanitarian aid funding, have drastically weakened the health system in North and South Kivu. Many health centres and hospitals are operating with reduced capacity due to a lack of staff, medicines, and equipment, while others have had to suspend their activities because of violence. In several areas, hundreds of thousands of people remain without regular access to healthcare. Population displacement caused by fighting also forces many families to seek refuge in forests or isolated areas, where exposure to mosquitoes is greater and access to healthcare is particularly limited. “While medical-humanitarian needs continue to grow in eastern DRC, we call on the authorities, donors, and all humanitarian actors to maintain and strengthen their investments in all responses to the many and diverse needs of the people,” adds Eckley. “It is vital that all emergencies be addressed simultaneously.” Footer Primary Menu Stories & news Stories from patients & staff News from our projects Statements & Opinion Media Coverage Our Work Where we work How we work Our medical approach Reports & Publications About Us MSF Australia Our Finances Get Involved Fundraise for us Social media Attend an event Workplace Giving Donate Now Ways to give Impact of your donation Supporter promise FAQs Update your details Follow Us

什么是埃博拉病毒?它如何传播?为什么刚果民主共和国的埃博拉疫情是一场“紧急事件”? - BBC

What is Ebola, how does it spread and why is DR Congo outbreak an 'emergency'? - BBC
新闻简报刚果民主共和国(DRC)目前的埃博拉病毒病暴发是该国有记录以来最致命的一次,截至2026年8月23日,已报告5,514例确诊病例和2,642例确诊死亡。此次暴发由埃博拉病毒的本迪布焦种引起,该种病毒已十余年未被发现。该毒株尤为棘手,原因在于缺乏已批准的抗病毒药物,且暴发地处于冲突区,流离失所的人群和边境通道松懈使得疫情控制更加复杂。该病毒通过接触感染者的体液传播,且最初的传播未被察觉数周,首例已知病例报告于2026年4月24日。DRC正准备推出针对扎伊尔种的Ervebo疫苗,作为试验的一部分,以评估其对本迪布焦种的有效性。世界卫生组织还在测试两种抗病毒药物(MBP134和瑞德西韦),以提高存活率。尽管采取了这些措施,此次暴发仍是一场重大公共卫生紧急事件,因其传播迅速,且该地区的不稳定局势和有限资源带来了严峻挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
此次暴发首次检测于2026年4月,首例已知病例报告于2026年4月24日。自2026年2月起,此次暴发已在未被察觉的情况下持续传播。截至2026年8月23日,已报告5,514例确诊病例和2,642例确诊死亡。
地点与对象
此次埃博拉病毒病暴发发生于刚果民主共和国,具体位于伊图里省,流行中心在布尼亚。受影响的人群包括该地区民众,尤其是通过接触感染者体液或在葬礼仪式期间暴露于病毒的人群。
事件
刚果民主共和国目前的埃博拉病毒病暴发是该国17次暴发中最致命的一次。此次暴发由埃博拉病毒的本迪布焦种引起,该种病毒已十余年未被发现。此次暴发被描述为有记录以来新发感染增长最快的一次。
规模、影响与风险
此次暴发传播迅速,病毒通过接触感染者体液传播。由于存在无已批准抗病毒药物的罕见本迪布焦种,且暴发地处于冲突区并有流离失所人群,风险进一步加剧。
应对、状态与不确定性
控制此次暴发的措施包括使用针对扎伊尔种的Ervebo疫苗,该疫苗正在测试其对本迪布焦种的有效性。世界卫生组织正在资助包括MBP134和瑞德西韦在内的抗病毒药物的临床试验。监测系统、密切接触者追踪和实验室基础设施已扩展。然而,最初的血液检测结果为阴性,因其设计用于识别更常见的病毒株。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

What is Ebola, how does it spread and why is DR Congo outbreak an 'emergency'? - BBC

News Brief
The current Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is the deadliest the country has faced, with 5,514 confirmed cases and 2,642 confirmed deaths as of 23 August 2026. The outbreak is caused by the Bundibugyo species of Ebola, which had not been seen for over a decade. This strain is particularly challenging due to the lack of an approved vaccine and the outbreak's location in a conflict zone, where displaced populations and porous borders complicate containment. The virus spreads through contact with infected bodily fluids, and the initial spread was undetected for weeks, with the first known case reported on 24 April 2026. The DRC is preparing to roll out the Ervebo vaccine, which targets the Zaire species, as part of a trial to assess its effectiveness against Bundibugyo. The World Health Organization is also testing two antiviral therapies, MBP134 and remdesivir, to improve survival rates. Despite these efforts, the outbreak remains a significant public health emergency due to its rapid spread and the challenges posed by the region's instability and limited resources.
Original source text (English)

Ireland Politics Scotland Scotland Politics Wales Wales Politics Africa Asia China India Australia Europe Latin America Middle East In Pictures BBC InDepth BBC Verify Sport Business World of Business Technology of Business NYSE Opening Bell Technology Watch Documentaries Artificial Intelligence Intelligence Revolution AI v the Mind Tech Now Health Watch Documentaries Culture Watch Documentaries Film & TV Music Art & Design Style Books Entertainment News Arts Watch Documentaries Arts in Motion Travel Watch Documentaries Destinations Africa Antarctica Asia Australia and Pacific Caribbean & Bermuda Central America Europe Middle East North America South America World’s Table Culture & Experiences Adventures The SpeciaList Earth Watch Documentaries Science Natural Wonders Climate Solutions Sustainable Business Green Living Audio Podcast Categories Radio Audio FAQs Video Watch Documentaries BBC Maestro Discover the World Live Live News Live Sport Documentaries Site search Home News Sport Business Technology Health Culture Arts Travel Earth Audio Video Live Documentaries Weather Newsletters Watch Live What is Ebola and why is stopping the latest outbreak so difficult? 10 hours ago Share Save Add as preferred on Google James Gallagher,Health and science correspondentand Emery Makumeno,BBC Africa, Kinshasa EPA The current Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo is now the deadliest of the 17 the country has faced as it continues to spread. United Nations experts have described it as the fastest growing outbreak on record in terms of new infections. Getting the spread of the deadly virus under control is challenging because it involves a rare species of Ebola for which there is no vaccine - though some are being tested - and the epicentre is in an area affected by conflict. What is Ebola and what are the symptoms? Ebola is a rare but deadly disease caused by a virus. Ebola viruses normally infect animals, typically fruit bats, but outbreaks among humans can sometimes start when people eat or handle infected animals. It takes two to 21 days for symptoms to appear. They come on suddenly and start like the flu or malaria, with fever, headache and tiredness. As the disease progresses, vomiting and diarrhoea develop and it can lead to organ failure. Some, but not all, patients develop internal and external bleeding. The virus spreads from one person to another by contact with infected bodily fluids such as blood or vomit. Ebola outbreaks used to be small and contained to remote rural areas. However, urbanisation is pushing larger populations closer to these natural reservoirs of Ebola and increasing the risk of transmission. Why is this Ebola outbreak different and is there a vaccine? This outbreak is caused by the rare Bundibugyo species of Ebola, which had not been seen for over a decade. Named after a district in Uganda where it was first detected, Bundibugyo has only caused two previous outbreaks - in 2007 and 2012. One study showed that it killed about a third of those infected, far less than the more common Zaire (66.6%) and Sudan (48.5%) species. About 48% of those known to have been infected in the current outbreak have died. Initial blood tests for Ebola in the affected areas were negative as they were designed to identify the more common form of the disease. In July, the UK's medicines regulator, the MHRA, said it had given permission for the first human trials of a vaccine for Bundibugyo to go ahead. It was created by a team from the University of Oxford based on the same technology used in the Oxford-AstraZeneca Covid vaccine. It took scientists just eight weeks to develop the new jab after plugging in genetic code from the Bundibugyo species of Ebola. The vaccine is being tested on 50 volunteers from Oxford aged 18 to 55 to see whether it triggers the correct antibody response without causing significant side effects. Three other groups are also developing different vaccines for Bundibugyo although they have not yet entered clinical trials. While treatments are available for other species of the Ebola virus, no drugs have been specifically approved to treat those infected with Bundibugyo. DR Congo is currently preparing to roll out the Ervebo vaccine - which helps protect against the Zaïre species of Ebola - as part of a trial to see if it is effective against the Bundibugyo species. The World Health Organization (WHO) is also sponsoring a separate clinical trial in the country to see whether two existing antiviral therapies can improve survival rates. The two drugs selected as the most promising are an experimental monoclonal antibody called MBP134 and remdesivir, an injection into a vein, which is also used to treat Covid. A further complication is that the outbreak is taking place in a conflict zone, with a quarter of million people displaced from their homes and people moving across porous borders into neighbouring countries. Recovery of Ebola patients offers rare moments of joy at epicentre of outbreak How health workers in DR Congo are treating Ebola and staying safe 'Speed, money and compassion' - lessons from an Ebola survivor and other experts How did the current Ebola outbreak start? The first known case was a nurse who developed symptoms on 24 April, which means the virus had been spreading undetected for weeks. The nurse died in Bunia, the capital of eastern DR Congo's Ituri province, according to Congolese Health Minister Samuel Roger Kamba. The victim's body was repatriated to Mongbwalu. Kamba said one of the reasons the virus spread so quickly was the number of people exposed to the body during the funeral ceremony. But researchers believe that it may have been spreading since February but it was initially misdiagnosed as malaria or typhoid. 'I buried my parents one day after the other' - Ebola mourners learn how to grieve safely How many Ebola cases have been reported and where are they? On 23 August, officials said there had been 5,514 confirmed cases and 2,642 confirmed deaths from the virus in DR Congo. There have also been 1,200 recoveries from Ebola so far, including four nurses whose discharge from hospital was celebrated at a special ceremony. Ituri province is the epicentre of this outbreak and accounts for the vast majority of confirmed infections. 1:43 Why DR Congo's Ebola outbreak is so hard to stop Cases have also been confirmed in North Kivu (714) and South Kivu (3), provinces partly controlled by the rebel AFC-M23 alliance. These discoveries signalled the outbreak's spread from its epicentre. The last patient being treated for Ebola in neighbouring Uganda was discharged from hospital on 16 July. Up to that point, the country had recorded 20 confirmed cases - with most having travelled from DR Congo - and two deaths. Uganda has since announced it is Ebola-free, going against the protocol of waiting 42 days - until 27 August - from when the last patient recovers to declare an outbreak over. What is being done in DR Congo to tackle the current Ebola outbreak? Getty Images The WHO and Congolese health authorities are urging residents to follow preventative measures to help limit the spread of Ebola Mass gatherings have been banned in the six provinces with confirmed cases - Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, Tshopo, Haut-Uele and Bas-Uele. The capital city, Kinshasa - which has no confirmed cases and is located some 1,800 km (1,100) miles from the outbreak - has also been ordered to ban mass gatherings. The government has now established four laboratories in Ituri - in Bunia, Mongbwalu, Beni and Aru - which can test blood samples for Bundibugyo and deliver results within 24 hours, removing earlier delays. Surveillance systems, contact tracing and the treatment infrastructure, with dedicated centres in several affected towns, have also been expanded, according to the health minister. The WHO has dedicated $3.9m (£2.9m) to tackling the outbreak, while Africa CDC has announced a $319m budget. A toll-free number, 151, has been provided for reporting symptoms and people are being reminded to: avoid contact with bodies of people who died with symptoms, or with dead animals not eat raw meat, as undercooked food may transmit the virus practise social distancing. How have the rebels responded to the latest Ebola outbreak? The AFC-M23 group has created an Ebola response team in an effort to prevent transmissions in the areas it controls. In late June, the rebel authorities had declared their zones Ebola-free, but introduced new restrictions in August, following two travellers testing positive in North Kivu. Shared taxis and passenger boats between government-held and rebel-held areas of the province, have been suspended, highlighting the hostilities between the two sides. The region's Ebola response co-ordinator Dr Freddy Kaniki, told the BBC in July that while UN agencies such as the WHO and Unicef - for children - had been helping, there has been no co-ordination with the Congolese government. What's the fighting in DR Congo all about? Trump's 'historic' peace deal for DR Congo shattered after rebels seize key city What are Rwanda and other countries doing about the Ebola outbreak? Rwanda closed its borders with DR Congo earlier on in the outbreak. According to Congolese media, authorities began to reopen parts of the border late in June following a lull in local infections. Uganda has temporarily suspended flights, buses and all other public transport crossing the border with DR Congo. Several other African countries have tightened border screenings and bolstered health facilities. The US has extended travel restrictions on its citizens who have been to DR Congo. Anyone who wants to return home must spend 21 days in a third country before being allowed into America. Go to BBCAfrica.com for more news from the African continent. Event

Second meeting of the IHR emergency committee on the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo - Temporary recommendations

News Brief
On 18 August 2026, the Emergency Committee regarding the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo met for the second time to reassess the situation, in line with International Health Regulations (2005).
Original source text (English)
On 18 August 2026, the Emergency Committee regarding the epidemic of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo met for the second time to reassess the situation, in line with International Health Regulations (2005)

B routine priorityPublic-health news

WHO: 'The current Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD) is the largest Ebola disease outbreak ever recorded in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), irrespective of Ebola virus species. On 17 May 2026, WHO declared the Bundibugyo virus disease outbre - news.cgtn.com

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) declared the Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda a Public Health Emergency of International Concern (PHEIC) on 17 May 2026. This outbreak is noted as the largest Ebola disease outbreak ever recorded in the DRC, regardless of the specific Ebola virus species. The overall risk remains very high within the DRC and high in neighboring countries, especially those sharing land borders, while being low at the regional and global levels due to preparedness and rapid detection measures. Health workers have been significantly affected, with 112 infected and 35 losing their lives. The WHO's declaration highlights the severity of the situation and the need for coordinated international efforts to contain the outbreak. The response includes ongoing investigation, testing, and public health measures to prevent further spread.
Original source text (Korean)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

刚果民主共和国埃博拉病毒病暴发100天:与疫情扩散赛跑——联合国新闻

100 days of Ebola and the race to stop the spread - UN News
新闻简报The Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has reached an unprecedented scale, with 5,515 confirmed cases and 2,642 deaths recorded as of 18 August 2026, resulting in a mortality rate of nearly 48 per cent. The outbreak affects six provinces, with Ituri province as the epicentre, accounting for 85 per cent of cases and 79 per cent of deaths. Despite significant progress, including the expansion of testing capacity to 19 laboratories and treatment beds to over 1,300, challenges persist due to ongoing clashes among armed groups, conflict-related displacement, and difficulties in accessing care. The WHO has emphasized the need for scaled-up action, improved community engagement, and tailored interventions to address local transmission dynamics. Additionally, 60 per cent of deaths occur outside treatment centres, highlighting the ongoing issues in early detection and swift referral to healthcare facilities. The UN is actively involved in the response, deploying experts, providing supplies, and supporting efforts to counter misinformation and improve access to care. The situation remains complex, with the outbreak unfolding amid a humanitarian crisis marked by malnutrition and lack of safe drinking water.
查看新闻五要素
时间
布尼迪布焦埃博拉病毒病暴发已持续100天,最新数据截至2026年8月18日。本报告发布日期为2026年8月24日。
地点与对象
The outbreak is occurring in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically in the eastern region, with Ituri province as the epicentre. It affects six provinces and has reached more than 2.5 million people through community engagement activities.
事件
The Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak has been ongoing for 100 days, with UN agencies warning about its unprecedented scale and the need for increased efforts to contain the spread. The outbreak is reported to have caused 5,515 cases and 2,642 deaths, with a mortality rate of almost 48 per cent.
规模、影响与风险
The outbreak has resulted in 5,515 confirmed cases and 2,642 deaths, with 60% of deaths occurring outside treatment centres. The mortality rate is 48 per cent, and the outbreak affects six provinces. The WHO has called for scaled-up action and highlighted the need for interventions tailored to local transmission dynamics.
应对、状态与不确定性
WHO has deployed over 260 experts, delivered over 330 tonnes of supplies, and allocated $5.6 million to emergency operations. The testing network has expanded to 19 laboratories, and treatment capacity has increased to over 1,300 beds. Contact tracing has improved to 84 per cent. However, challenges remain due to insecurity, displacement, and difficulties in accessing care. The response is ongoing, and the situation is described as compounded by conflict-related issues.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

100 days of Ebola and the race to stop the spread - UN News

News Brief
The Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has reached an unprecedented scale, with 5,515 confirmed cases and 2,642 deaths recorded as of 18 August 2026, resulting in a mortality rate of nearly 48 per cent. The outbreak affects six provinces, with Ituri province as the epicentre, accounting for 85 per cent of cases and 79 per cent of deaths. Despite significant progress, including the expansion of testing capacity to 19 laboratories and treatment beds to over 1,300, challenges persist due to ongoing clashes among armed groups, conflict-related displacement, and difficulties in accessing care. The WHO has emphasized the need for scaled-up action, improved community engagement, and tailored interventions to address local transmission dynamics. Additionally, 60 per cent of deaths occur outside treatment centres, highlighting the ongoing issues in early detection and swift referral to healthcare facilities. The UN is actively involved in the response, deploying experts, providing supplies, and supporting efforts to counter misinformation and improve access to care. The situation remains complex, with the outbreak unfolding amid a humanitarian crisis marked by malnutrition and lack of safe drinking water.
